This is a security noninterference proof for STLC with if-then-else, based on the "Logical Relations for Security" / "Security Noninterference" benchmark. This appears to be from the "sledgehammer"/"Rocq" benchmarks. Let me think about how to prove this.

This is a substantial proof. Let me look at similar known developments. The problem is from the "SLam" calculus benchmark, likely derived from a paper "Secure Compilation" or similar. The theorem is termination-insensitive noninterference for a language with security levels, subtyping, protection, and if-then-else.

Let me check the environment: Rocq version, available libraries.

Let me plan the proof. We need a logical relation (binary, probably "noninterference" relation) over terms parameterized by a relation on labels / security levels.

The classic approach for such benchmarks (I recall the "STLC noninterference" tasks from the "Rocq proof benchmark" e.g., from the "Neural theorem proving" datasets) is:

Define a logical relation over values and terms:
- `Rel_T (T : ty) (kappa : security)`? Actually with security annotations, the relation is indexed by an "observer level" or by a "security" giving reader and indirect_reader.

Given the structure: `has_type empty t T_secret`, two terms at same type. We need: if `security_of T_secret.(indirect_reader)` cannot flow to `security_of T_result.(indirect_reader)`, then results erased equal.

The standard proof: define a binary relation on terms `t1 ~_{T, kappa} t2` where `kappa` is the observer security level (indirect reader), meaning the observer with indirect-reader level kappa cannot distinguish t1 and t2. Actually the relation should be indexed by the "observer level" `l` (a label). The relation `R_T^l(t1,t2)` says t1 and t2 are indistinguishable at type T to an observer whose indirect reader is l.

Key idea: if `l` is Low (public observer), then t1 and t2 must erase to the same value (for ground types). If l is High, anything goes.

More precisely, the logical relation is defined with respect to a label `l` representing the observer's security level (indirect_reader). The type's security annotation `kappa` gives a reader level and indirect reader level. For a value of type T with security kappa:
- if kappa.reader flows to l? Hmm.

Actually, the "flowsto" relation: `flows_to l1 l2 = False` only when l1 = High, l2 = Low. So `flows_to a b` means information at level a may flow to level b (High -> High yes, Low -> High yes, Low -> Low yes, High -> Low no).

The noninterference condition: `~ flows_to (security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader) (security_of T_result).(indirect_reader)`. Since only High -> Low is false, this means T_secret.indirect_reader = High and T_result.indirect_reader = Low.

So essentially the theorem says: two terms of a secret type (indirect_reader High) produce equal erased results in a context of public result type (indirect_reader Low). Combined with ground and transparent constraints.

Transparent T_result means: `security_le (security_of T_result) (security_of T_result)` — wait that's `transparent_at (security_of T_result) T_result` and requires `security_le (security_of T) kappa` where kappa = security_of T, trivially true by reflexivity of flows_to (flows_to l l is True for both Low and High). Hmm, but `transparent_at` recurses into components with the same kappa: for `Ty_Sum T1 T2 _`, requires `transparent_at kappa T1` where kappa = security_of (T_result), not of T1. And `security_le (security_of T1) kappa` etc. So transparency means all subcomponents' security is below the top-level security, i.e., all sub-values' reader and indirect readers flow to the result's kappa levels.

Hmm wait, the `transparent` definition uses `security_of T` where T is the top type; recursive calls use the same kappa. So `transparent T` requires for each subterm U: security_le (security_of U) (security_of T). This allows transferring protections.

The relation should be defined such that for type T and observer level `l : label` (which is under the "indirect reader" ordering?), we need:

- If observer level l = Low (public), the relation demands erased equality of values (for ground types), and semantic equality of functions for arrow types.
- If observer level l = High (secret), the relation is True (all terms related) since secret observer can see everything. Hmm, but careful: an observer at High can distinguish anything, so the relation for High should be True? Wait, noninterference at High observer is trivial. But in the fundamental theorem, we need to handle subtyping and protection which mixes levels. Let's think.

Standard approach for termination-insensitive noninterference in STLC with security levels, following e.g. "simple rel" from the challenge:

`R l T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, value v1 -> value v2 -> t1 -->* v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> V l T v1 v2`? Or define directly on values.

Actually let's define value relation `V l T v1 v2` and expression relation `E l T t1 t2 := forall v1, t1 -->* v1 -> value v1 -> exists v2, t2 -->* v2 /\ value v2 /\ V l T v1 v2`. Hmm but for termination-insensitive NI with possibly nonterminating terms, the usual definition is: if t1 evaluates to v1 then t2 evaluates to v2 with related values, AND conversely? For same_result we only need: both evaluations complete → erased values equal. So we need at least one direction... but to compose, we need both directions (the relation must be symmetric). Since same_result requires equality given any completed evaluations of both, we need: for any evaluation of t1 to v1 and t2 to v2, erased v1 = erased v2. That's an equality statement, so we can define the relation as a "logical equivalence" ensuring that if t1 evaluates then its value is related to any value t2 evaluates to... Let's design:

`E l T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> value v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v2 -> V l T v1 v2` where `V` is symmetric. For Ty_Sum/Prod, need recursion: for sums, V Low (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa) v1 v2 requires they are both tm_inl with related payloads or both tm_inr with related payloads (matching sides!), which gives erase equality via IH. For prod, both pairs with components related, IH gives erased equal components. Good.

Subtyping lemma (value relation monotonicity): If subtype T U and V obs (shape_of T) T v1 v2 then V obs (shape_of U) U v1 v2? But wait, T and U may have different shapes? Subtyping preserves shape! Coq's `induction s` gives only IHs for s1,s2 of the *current lemma*, not the other lemma. Standard solution: prove a combined statement by induction on s:

```
Lemma V_props : forall s obs,
  (forall T U v1 v2, shape_of T = s -> shape_of U = s -> subtype T U ->
     V obs s T v1 v2 -> V obs s U v1 v2) /\
  (forall l0 l1 T U v1 v2, shape_of T = s -> shape_of U = s -> subtype T U ->
     flows_to l0 l1 -> V obs s (ty_protect l0 T) v1 v2 -> V obs s (ty_protect l1 U) v1 v2).
Proof.
  induction s; intros obs; split; ...
```
In each case, the IH is `V_props s1 obs` and `V_props s2 obs`, giving both properties at smaller shapes.

Now, what about the forward protection lemma: `V at T v1 v2 -> V at ty_protect l T (protect_value v1 l) (protect_value v2 l)`. Protect_value changes runtime annotations, which matters for arrow values. For non-arrow T:

(D) Fundamental theorem with environments. Let's flesh it out at obs=Low only.

Define:
```
Definition V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop := ...  (* Fixpoint on shape/type *)
```
We can define V directly by Fixpoint on T since we don't need ty_protect recursion? In the arrow case, we call V at ty_protect k.ind T2. At obs=Low, ty_protect k.ind T2 is either T2 (if k.ind = Low) or opaque (ind High) with V = True. But the definition can't branch on k.ind and use V T2 in the Low case? It can: `if flows_to k.ind Low then V T2 ... else True`? We can define V by recursion on T and in the arrow case use V at T2 (structurally smaller), gated by whether k.ind = Low:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low => (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inl w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 k2 /\ V T1 w1 w2)
               \/ (exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_inr w1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 k2 /\ V T2 w1 w2)
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 k =>
      match k.ind with
      | Low => exists w1 w2 k1 k2, v1 = tm_pair w1 w2 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair w1 w2 k2 /\ V T1 w1 w2 /\ V T2 w1 w2
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      match k.ind with
      | Low =>
          exists body1 ka1 body2 ka2,
            v1 = tm_abs T1 body1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_abs T1 body2 ka2 /\
            security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\
            (forall w1 w2 r, value w1 -> value w2 -> V T1 w1 w2 ->
               flows_to k.reader r ->
               forall u1 u2,
                 tm_app v1 w1 r -->* u1 -> value u1 ->
                 tm_app v2 w2 r -->* u2 -> value u2 ->
                 V T2 u1 u2)   (* since ty_protect k.ind T2 = T2 at ind Low *)
      | High => True
      end
  end.
```
Wait: for arrows, `v1 = tm_abs T1 body1 ka1`: the type annotation T1 in the value should match the type? Values carry `tm_abs T body kappa` with T the argument type. The relation at Arrow T1 T2 k: v1 might be tm_abs T1' body1 ka1 with T1' not syntactically equal to T1 (e.g., subtype T1' <: T1). Our strict clause above forces T1' = T1, too strong! In the T_Abs case, v = tm_abs T1 body kappa where T1 is exactly the type from T_Abs, so fine. But in V_monotone S_Arrow, V at Arrow A0 B0 k0 gives v = tm_abs A0 body ka; transported to Arrow A B k where A ≠ A0 (domain contravariance) — the value's stored domain type is still A0, not A. So requiring equality with A would fail! Need to handle the stored type: the value tm_abs T_body body ka stores the parameter type. The relation at Arrow A B k should require the stored type to be *subtype-related* to A? The value's actual parameter type A0 must satisfy ? The stored type is used in... the step rule ST_AppAbs doesn't check types at runtime; the stored type only matters for locally_closed? No, value (tm_abs T body kappa) requires locally_closed, which ignores T. Typing of the value at Arrow A B k via T_Abs gives stored type exactly the type's domain, then T_Sub can change the type. So a value of type Arrow A B k can have stored type A0 with subtype A0 A? Let's check S_Arrow contravariance: Arrow A0 B0 k0 <: Arrow A B k requires subtype A A0 (A supertype's domain is subtype of A0). So A <: A0, i.e., the stored domain A0 is a *supertype* of the type's domain A. So relation at Arrow A B k should accept stored types A0 with subtype A A0. The runtime behavior of the body depends on how the argument value w is used; the body was typed with parameter type A0. The relation V at T1's clause applies to arguments related at A (the type's domain); to apply the function's body relation, we'd need arguments related at A0. Hmm, but in the arrow clause, do we need the stored type at all? The clause is semantic: for args w1,w2 related at A, flow flows_to k.reader r, evaluations of app v1 w1 r → V at B. This doesn't mention stored type. So we can drop the stored-type requirement entirely! Just require v1 = tm_abs _ body1 ka1 and v2 = tm_abs _ body2 ka2 (any stored types), security_le ka1 k, security_le ka2 k, and the behavioral clause for args at A. Then in T_Abs case, stored type is T1 and args at T1; body relation via IH works. In V_monotone S_Arrow, the behavior clause transfers as analyzed (arguments at A → V_monotone to A0 contravariantly). Good. But wait: the T_Abs case's behavioral clause needs the stored type for the body substitution: the body is typed with parameter type T1, so open body w is typed at T2 when w has type T1 (environment). The IH gives E at T2 for subst w. The relation at the stored type T1 is used through env_rel. Fine; stored type doesn't appear in V clause.

But there's a subtle issue: In V_monotone S_Arrow, the behavioral clause of V at U expects args related at A and evaluations of app v1 w1 r. We transfer to V at T which expects args related at A0. We get V at A0 from V at A by contravariance: subtype A A0, V_monotone gives V at A → V at A0. Good. So no stored-type constraint needed.

What about the T_Abs case's `security_le ka k`: ka = k, holds reflexively. Good.

But hmm, `security_le ka1 k` requires *both* reader and indirect components flow. Reflexivity: flows_to x x True for both labels; definitional by cases. Fine.

Now, does V need the `exists body1 ka1` form, or can we allow any v1 and just require evaluations of app to behave? If v1 is not an abs, the behavioral clause may still be provable or vacuous. To prove the forward/monotone lemmas, having the abs form is convenient. In T_Abs, yes. In V_monotone, V at subtype gives abs form and security_le ka k0; transport to k via transitivity. Good. In T_App, the function value from E at arrow type gives V arrow, which gives abs form; but do we actually need the abs form in T_App? The arrow clause is applied directly, no need to deconstruct. In the protection rule (T_Protect with l), if l=Low we need evaluations of tm_protect Low t; the E at arrow gives V; nothing to do with abs. So the exists form is only used by V_monotone. Fine.

Now, E relation:
```
Definition E (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> value v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v2 -> V T v1 v2.
```
Note: E does not require values to be un-stuck or anything.

Environment relation for substitutions. Since we only care about obs=Low, define:
```
Definition env_rel (Gamma : context) (sigma1 sigma2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> E T (sigma1 x) (sigma2 x).
```
Fundamental theorem:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma1 sigma2,
    env_rel Gamma sigma1 sigma2 ->
    E T (subst_env sigma1 t) (subst_env sigma2 t).
```

Proof by induction on the typing derivation. Let's go rule by rule.

Notation: s_i = subst_env sigma_i t.

- T_Var: lookup x Gamma = Some T. env_rel gives E T (sigma1 x) (sigma2 x). subst_env sigma (fvar x) = sigma x. Exact.
- T_Unit: t = tm_unit kappa; subst = tm_unit kappa. E: for all v1 v2, multi (unit) v1 → value v1, ... Need multi_value: if multi (tm_unit k) v and value v then v = tm_unit k? Actually multi from a value v to u with value u implies u = v (values are normal and deterministic? multi_refl gives u=v; any step from value impossible). Prove `multi_value_eq : forall v u, value v -> multi v u -> value u -> u = v`. Then V T v1 v2 where T = Ty_Unit kappa: True. Wait, V at Ty_Unit is True regardless. So E regardless of v1,v2 forms? E's conclusion is V (Ty_Unit kappa) v1 v2 = True. So no need for value eq! Because V at unit is True for arbitrary v1,v2. But E's hypotheses: t1 = tm_unit, t2 = tm_unit; V unit True. Yes, immediate.

Since E quantifies over evaluations of well-typed terms, the values should indeed be units; the typing induction should establish V if V unit requires shape. So V at T1 protect_value w1 High would FAIL. But is the actual runtime behavior consistent? But typing says kappa.ind Low; subtype gives ka.ind ≤ kappa.ind (wf?) — wait, can ka.ind = High while kappa.ind = Low? security_le ka kappa requires ka.ind ≤ kappa.ind, so no. So the invariant security_le ka kappa prevents this. Good: T_Fst with kappa.ind Low implies ka.ind Low, so protect_value w1 Low = w1, no shift. Nice!

  So including `security_le ka kappa` for runtime annotations in V prevents all protection shifts at obs=Low! Because whenever a visible type has ind Low and runtime annotation ka with ka.ind ≤ kappa.ind = Low, we get ka.ind = Low, so protection at ka.ind is identity. Great. This is the crucial insight.

  - T_Snd analogous.
- T_Inl/T_Inr: t = tm_inl t0 kappa, T = Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa. Case kappa.ind:

  Low: ty_protect Low T' = T'. Evaluations of tm_case (subst t0) bodies r to u1: decompose: subst t0 -->* c' value c'; then case c' ... -->* u.   Need E (ty_protect l T0) (protect l s1) (protect l s2).

Now V's definition matches on `(security_of T).indirect_reader` for each constructor. Let me write V by matching on T and the security annotation:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low => exists k1 k2, v1 = tm_unit k1 /\ v2 = tm_unit k2 /\
                 security_le k1 k /\ security_le k2 k
      end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low => (exists a1 a2 ka1 ka2, v1 = tm_inl a1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 ka2 /\
                   security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\ V T1 a1 a2)
            \/ (exists a1 a2 ka1 ka2, v1 = tm_inr a1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 ka2 /\
                   security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\ V T2 a1 a2)
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low => exists a1 a2 b1 b2 ka1 ka2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 ka2 /\
                 security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\ V T1 a1 a2 /\ V T2 b1 b2
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low => exists Ta1 body1 ka1 Ta2 body2 ka2,
                 v1 = tm_abs Ta1 body1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_abs Ta2 body2 ka2 /\
                 security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\
                 forall w1 w2 r, value w1 -> value w2 -> V T1 w1 w2 ->
                   flows_to k.(reader) r ->
                   forall u1 u2,
                     tm_app v1 w1 r -->* u1 -> value u1 ->
                     tm_app v2 w2 r -->* u2 -> value u2 -> V T2 u1 u2
      end
  end.
```

Hmm, the arrow clause as written quantifies over w1,w2 with `value w1` etc. But note in V_subtype, when transferring the behavioral clause from V at T to V at U, the arguments and label are the same; fine.

Now, potential issue: the behavioral clause's conclusion V T2 u1 u2 has no protection, correct for k.ind Low. But what if kappa.ind Low while T2's ind High: V T2 = True, so clause trivial. If T2 ind Low: strict. Fine.

Another potential issue: In T_App, the arrow type's kappa is the annotation from the typing rule. But t1's type is exactly Arrow T1 T2 kappa. The IH E at that type gives V at it for evaluated function values. Good.

Now, what about ST_AppAbs's requirement `value (tm_abs T body kappa)` and `value v`: In the behavioral clause we have `value w1`, and v1 = tm_abs Ta1 body1 ka1; we need value v1. Does V at arrow give value v1? Not explicitly. In the T_Abs case, the E hypothesis gives value v1. But in V_subtype, the V hypothesis's abs form doesn't include value-ness. The behavioral clause is a Prop we can use; when proving V at U's behavioral clause by applying V at T's, we don't need to re-prove value v1; we just transfer. The only place we *use* the behavioral clause to derive an actual step is in T_App/E, where the evaluated values have value hypotheses from E. But when applying V arrow in T_App, we have v1 from E's evaluation with `value v1`. So fine. In T_Abs's proof of the behavioral clause, we need value v1 where v1 is the abstraction; we get it from E's value hypothesis after multi_value_eq. Good.
  But V at arrow might be established for values not known to be values (e.g., via V_subtype with V hypothesis from an opaque type? No, opaque gives True and then we can't prove strict at U unless U opaque). Actually V_subtype from V at T to V at U: if V at T is True because T opaque, but U visible? security_le T U with U.ind Low and T.ind High impossible. So V at U strict always comes from V at T strict, which includes abs forms. Fine.
  So we don't need value-ness in V.

Now, V_to_E_value: values normal. Need multi_value lemma:
```
Lemma value_no_step : forall v, value v -> forall u, ~ (v --> u).
```
Actually to show multi v u with value u implies u = v, we can prove `value_step_impossible` and `multi_value_inv`: if value v and v -->* u and value u then u = v. Proof by induction on multi: refl; step case: step v t2 contradicts value_no_step. 
And value_no_step: destruct value; intro step; inversion step. For v_abs: step (abs) cannot. For pair: steps require component steps; pairs of values have no component steps; but value (pair v1 v2) gives value v1,v2; inversion on ST_Pair1 would need v1 --> t1' contradicting IH value v1; similarly. For inl/inr: IH. For unit: no step rule. We can prove by induction on the value derivation and inversion on step. Or use the (deterministic) step relation. Let's write:
```
Lemma value_no_step : forall v, value v -> forall u, ~ step v u.
Proof.
  intros v Hv. induction Hv; intros u Hstep; inversion Hstep; subst; eauto.
Qed.
```
For v_abs: inversion Hstep (no rule matches abs as source). For pair: inversion gives ST_Pair1/2 requiring step v1 ..., and IHHv1 or IHHv2 contradicts. For inl: ST_Inl requires step v t', contradict IHHv. For inr similarly. For unit: no rule.
Need to ensure inversion clears. We'll do it in Coq and adjust.

Then multi_value_eq:
```
Lemma multi_value_eq : forall v u, value v -> multi v u -> value u -> u = v.
Proof. intros v u Hv Hm. induction Hm. reflexivity. exfalso; eapply value_no_step; eauto. 
```
Wait induction on multi with v fixed: multi_refl u=v; step case: step v t2 then multi t2 t3; value v contradicts step v t2. Good.

Also for decomposition lemmas we need: if multi (tm_app a b r) u and value u, then ...; the standard approach via induction on multi:
```
Lemma app_multi_decomp : forall a b r u,
  tm_app a b r -->* u -> value u ->
  exists a' b', a -->* a' /\ value a' /\ b -->* b' /\ value b' /\ tm_app a' b' r -->* u.
```
Induction on multi:
- multi_refl: a'=a, b'=b; need a -->* a (refl), value a, b -->* b, value b, u = app a b r -->* u refl. But we need value a and value b; from value u = value (tm_app a b r) — impossible! app is not a value. So this case is contradictory. Need `value_app_absurd : ~ value (tm_app a b r)` by inversion on value. Use exfalso.
- multi_step: step (app a b r) t2, multi t2 u. Invert step:
  - ST_AppAbs: a = tm_abs T body kappa, b = v value, flows...; t2 = tm_protect kappa.ind (open body v). Then we need to show exists a' b' with a -->* a', value a', b -->* b', value b', app a' b' r -->* u. Take a'=a (value abs from hypothesis), b'=v? Is v = b; b is a value (hypothesis), and t2 = protect... multi to u value. We need app a' b' r -->* u. But app (abs) b r --> tm_protect ... via ST_AppAbs, then multi to u. So app a b r -->* u by multi_step ST_AppAbs (multi t2 u). Good. Need a -->* a refl and value a from ST_AppAbs value hypothesis; b -->* b refl and value b. Done.
  - ST_App1: a --> a', t2 = app a' b r. Apply IH to multi (app a' b r) u value u → exists a'' b', a' -->* a'', ..., app a'' b' r -->* u. Then a -->* a'' by multi_step Hstep (a' -->* a''), b -->* b', app a'' b' r -->* u. Done.
  - ST_App2: value a, b --> b', t2 = app a b' r. Apply IH → a'' b'', ...; then a -->* a'' directly (from IH), b -->* b'' by multi_step Hstep. Done.
  Maybe simpler: prove decomposition in two stages: first "app a b r -->* u with value u" implies a evaluates to value, then b, then app values. The above is fine.

Similarly `pair_multi_decomp`, `inl_multi_decomp`, `inr_multi_decomp`, `fst_multi_decomp`, `snd_multi_decomp`, `case_multi_decomp`, `protect_multi_decomp`. That's a lot of boilerplate but straightforward.

Maybe we can avoid some by a general lemma: the step relation is deterministic, and a term's multi to a value can be characterized by evaluation of subterms. Another approach: prove a general "multi-step decomposition" using the fact that step is deterministic and functions are evaluated left-to-right; but writing the specific lemmas is fine.

Let's list the needed decomposition lemmas:
- value_app_absurd, value_pair? pairs are values; value_inl? values.
- app_multi_decomp: above.
- pair_multi_decomp: multi (tm_pair a b k) u, value u → exists a' b', a -->* a' ∧ value a' ∧ b -->* b' ∧ value b' ∧ u = tm_pair a' b' k.
- inl_multi_decomp / inr_multi_decomp: multi (tm_inl a k) u, value u → exists a', a -->* a' ∧ value a' ∧ u = tm_inl a' k. (Similarly inr.)
- fst_multi_decomp: multi (tm_fst t r) u, value u → exists t', t -->* t' ∧ value t' ∧ tm_fst t' r -->* u. Actually we then need t' to be a pair to step; in T_Fst the E at prod gives t' as pair with payloads. So a two-level decomp: first evaluate t to a value t', then the fst of the value (a pair) steps. Let's define: `fst_multi_decomp : tm_fst t r -->* u -> value u -> exists t', t -->* t' /\ value t' /\ tm_fst t' r -->* u.` Then in T_Fst, after getting t' and V prod t' t'', we know t' = tm_pair ... (from V strict), and then we need to evaluate tm_fst (pair ...) r; the step is ST_FstPair then protect; we can further decompose `fst_pair_multi_decomp` or handle directly: tm_fst (tm_pair w1 w2 ka) r -->* u implies (flow holds) tm_protect ka.ind w1 -->* u. Prove by "inversion at the head": since pair is a value, ST_FstPair applies; multi from fst pair is either refl (but fst pair is not a value) or step ST_FstPair. So we get tm_protect ... -->* u. Let's prove `fst_multi_decomp` returning the residue, then a lemma:
  `fst_multi_pair : forall w1 w2 ka r u, flows_to ka.reader r -> tm_fst (tm_pair w1 w2 ka) r -->* u -> tm_protect ka.ind w1 -->* u`.
  Proof by induction on multi? The term tm_fst (pair) is not a value, so multi must start with a step; inversion gives ST_FstPair (since pair is value and the step rules: ST_Fst requires t --> t' with t = pair; contradict value_no_step) → t2 = tm_protect ka.ind w1; then multi t2 u. Done.
  
  Similarly `snd_multi_pair`.
- case_multi_decomp: tm_case t b1 b2 r -->* u, value u → exists t', t -->* t' ∧ value t' ∧ tm_case t' b1 b2 r -->* u. Then for the value t' = inl a ka: `case_inl_multi : flows_to ka.reader r -> tm_case (tm_inl a ka) b1 b2 r -->* u -> tm_protect ka.ind (open b1 a) -->* u` (inversion at head: ST_CaseLeft applies; ST_Case requires inl to step, contradiction). Similarly inr.
- protect_multi_decomp: tm_protect l t -->* u, value u → exists v, t -->* v ∧ value v ∧ u = protect_value v l. Wait need also that protect_value v l is a value to ensure multi from protect_value v l to u implies u = ...; the decomp can say multi (tm_protect l t) u with value u → exists v, t -->* v ∧ value v ∧ u = protect_value v l. Proof by induction on multi:
  - refl: u = protect l t is a value → absurd (value_protect_absurd).
  - step: invert step: ST_ProtectValue (value t) → t2 = protect_value t l; multi t2 u; t2 is a value (prove value_protect_value: value v -> value (protect_value v l)); then multi_value_eq gives u = t2; exists t; t -->* t refl; value t from rule. 
    ST_Protect (t --> t') → t2 = protect l t'; IH on multi t2 u gives v, t' -->* v, value v, u = protect_value v l; then t -->* v by multi_step. Done.
- app with abs: after app_multi_decomp gives app a' b' r -->* u with a' value; in T_App, V arrow gives a' = abs; then need `app_abs_multi` decomposition: tm_app (tm_abs T body ka) w r -->* u, value w, flows_to ka.reader r → tm_protect ka.ind (open body w) -->* u. Inversion at the head: the term is app abs w r; step rules: ST_AppAbs (if value abs and value w and flow) → directly; ST_App1 requires abs --> t', contradict value_no_step; ST_App2 requires w --> t', contradict value_no_step. So the first multi step must be ST_AppAbs. Good.

- For T_Protect Low: protect_multi_decomp gives u = protect_value v Low; prove protect_value_low : protect_value v Low = v. Now what about T_App when kappa.ind High: result type ty_protect High T2 has ind High → V True. E at that type is trivial regardless. Let's now also think about V_subtype more carefully with the *annotation bound* fields and the arrow behavioral clause.

Statement:
```
Lemma V_subtype : forall T U, subtype T U -> forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2.
```
Proof by induction on the subtype derivation (using `induction 1` maybe with general v1 v2). Let's write it manually.

Case S_Unit k1 k2 Hwf1 Hwf2 Hle:
  Need V (Ty_Unit k2) v1 v2 from V (Ty_Unit k1).
  destruct k2.ind.
  - High: V high true.
  - Low: Hle gives flows_to k1.ind k2.ind; k2.ind Low; flows_to k1.ind Low → k1.ind = Low (since flows_to High Low false). Destruct k1.ind eqn:E; if High, Hle : flows_to High Low = False → contradiction; if Low, unfold V k1 gives exists k1' k2', v1=unit, v2=unit, security_le k1' k1, security_le k2' k1; need security_le k1' k2: trans with Hle. 
  We can write helper lemmas: `flows_to_low_r : flows_to l Low <-> l = Low`; `flows_to_anything_high : flows_to l High`. Might ease.

Case S_Sum: similar, using IH on component subtypes (which are subderivations: `induction Hsub` gives IHs).
  In the Low k2 case, need k1.ind Low from Hle and k2.ind Low. V at sum k1 strict gives inl/inr with security_le ka k1 and V T1 a1 a2. Transport security_le ka k2 via Hle (security_le is transitive). Payload V transported by IH (on subtype T1 U1). Good.

Case S_Prod: analogous.

Case S_Arrow: as analyzed. Let's detail:
  T = Arrow T1 T2 k1, U = Arrow U1 U2 k2, with Hdom : subtype U1 T1, Hcod : subtype T2 U2, Hle : security_le k1 k2.
  Goal V (Arrow U1 U2 k2) v1 v2. destruct k2.ind.
  - High: V_high.
  - Low: need strict. Hle gives k1.ind ≤ k2.ind = Low, so k1.ind Low. Unfold V (Arrow T1 T2 k1) (k1.ind Low) from HV: exists Ta1 body1 ka1 Ta2 body2 ka2; v1 = tm_abs Ta1 body1 ka1; v2 = abs ...; security_le ka1 k1; security_le ka2 k1; behavior Hbeh: forall w1 w2 r, value w1 -> value w2 -> V T1 w1 w2 -> flows_to k1.reader r -> forall u1 u2, app v1 w1 r -->* u1 -> value u1 -> app v2 w2 r -->* u2 -> value u2 -> V T2 u1 u2.
    Need: exists ... same witnesses; security_le ka_i k2 by trans; behavior for U: forall w1 w2 r, value w1 -> value w2 -> V U1 w1 w2 -> flows_to k2.reader r -> evals -> V U2 u1 u2.
    Given V U1 w1 w2, get V T1 w1 w2 by IH Hdom (contravariant: subtype U1 T1, V U1 → V T1). IH on Hdom is available.
    Flow: Hle gives k1.reader ≤ k2.reader; flows_to k2.reader r → flows_to k1.reader r by trans.
    Apply Hbeh → V T2 u1 u2. Then IH Hcod : subtype T2 U2 gives V U2 u1 u2. Done.
  Note: induction on subtype derivation: in S_Arrow case, IHs for Hdom and Hcod are available. Good.

Case S_Trans T U V: IH1 : V T → V U; IH2 : V U → V V? Careful with variable naming in Coq: `induction H` where H : subtype T U; S_Trans case has T U V0 and derivations subtype T U, subtype U V0. IHs: forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2 and forall v1 v2, V U v1 v2 -> V V0 v1 v2. Then goal V T → V V0 by composition. Good.

But wait: in S_Sum, the subtype derivation's *shape*: we need to know T and U are sums; `induction H` with H : subtype T U will give cases where T,U are constructors. In S_Trans, T,U arbitrary; use IH. Fine. However, the induction hypothesis in S_Sum for subtype T1 U1 needs to be applied with v1,v2 as the *payloads*, but the IH is `forall v1 v2, V T1 v1 v2 -> V U1 v1 v2`? If we do `induction H` with H : subtype T U and the goal `forall v1 v2, V T v1 v2 -> V U v1 v2`, Coq's induction will generalize v1 v2 if we set it up as `intros T U H; induction H; intros v1 v2 HV`. Then each case has IHs for the premises. For S_Sum: IHsubtype1 : forall v1 v2, V T1 v1 v2 -> V U1 v1 v2, etc. Good.

Now the fundamental theorem's T_Abs case requires `V (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa)` and the behavioral clause. Let's detail the Coq proof:

We're proving `E (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) (subst sigma1 (tm_abs T1 body kappa)) (subst sigma2 ...)`. Wait, E is defined as a forall; after intros v1 v2 Hmulti1 Hval1 Hmulti2 Hval2, prove V (Arrow ...) v1 v2.
- subst_env sigma (tm_abs T1 body kappa) = tm_abs T1 (subst_env sigma body) kappa, by definitional unfolding of subst_env.
- From Hmulti1 : multi (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma1 body) kappa) v1 and Hval1, use multi_value_eq with value of the abs? multi_value_eq requires `value (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma1 body) kappa)`, which we don't have (lc unknown). Hmm. We can't apply multi_value_eq without value of the source. We only know the source is an abs term; does multi from an abs term (possibly not lc) reach a value? Steps: abs is not a redex, no step rule has tm_abs as the source at the top level. So any multi from tm_abs ... must be multi_refl regardless of lc! Because step t t' requires t to match a redex; tm_abs doesn't match any left-hand side of step (ST_AppAbs has app; others match other constructors). So prove `multi_abs_inv : forall T body k u, multi (tm_abs T body k) u -> u = tm_abs T body k` by induction on multi: refl; step case: `step (tm_abs ...) t2` is impossible; prove `abs_no_step`. Then Hmulti1 gives v1 = tm_abs ... So no lc needed for that. Then Hval1 gives value (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma1 body) kappa), which includes lc. Good.
- Then destruct kappa.ind.
  - High: V_high.
  - Low: construct exists Ta1 body1 ka1 Ta2 body2 ka2. Take Ta1 = T1, body1 = subst sigma1 body, ka1 = kappa; similarly 2. Need v1 = tm_abs T1 (subst sigma1 body) kappa (from Hmulti1 inversion), security_le kappa kappa (reflexive), and the behavioral clause.
    Behavioral clause: intros w1 w2 r Hvalw1 Hvalw2 HVw Hflow. Need forall u1 u2, app v1 w1 r -->* u1 -> value u1 -> app v2 w2 r -->* u2 -> value u2 -> V T2 u1 u2.
    Rewrite v1 to tm_abs T1 (subst sigma1 body) kappa (Hmulti1 inversion) and same v2. Then intros u1 u2 Happ1 Hvalu1 Happ2 Hvalu2.
    We need to decompose Happ1: app (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma1 body) kappa) w1 r -->* u1 with value w1 and flow flows_to kappa.reader r. Use app_abs_multi to get Hprot1 : tm_protect kappa.ind (open (subst sigma1 body) w1) -->* u1 (we're in kappa.ind Low case, but the lemma is general). Similarly Hprot2.
    Now choose a fresh x ∉ L ∪ fv body. Careful: x also must be fresh from? We need the body typing premise with x; it holds for x ∉ L. Since we can choose x afresh in the proof, we can pick x not in L ∪ fv body. But we need to *instantiate* the premise: Hbody : forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open body (fvar x)) T2. For our chosen x with x ∉ L, Hbody x HxL.
    Define sigma1' := fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then w1 else sigma1 y; sigma2' similarly.
    Need env_rel (update Gamma x T1) sigma1' sigma2'. This requires lc of sigma1' values; for y=x, w1 is a value, and values are locally closed? value_abs requires lc; v_unit trivially; pair: recursively; inl/inr: recursively. So prove `value_lc : value v -> locally_closed v`. For y≠x, env_rel Gamma sigma1 gives lc (we put lc into env_rel). For the E part: for y=x need E T1 w1 w2: from V T1 w1 w2 and values via V_to_E.
    Then IHbody := IH (the induction hypothesis for the body typing derivation) applied to sigma1' sigma2' env_rel': gives E T2 (subst sigma1' (open body (fvar x))) (subst sigma2' (...)).
    Then use subst_env_lc to rewrite:
      subst sigma1' (open body (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma1 body) w1.
    Proof: subst_env_lc sigma1' Hlc' 0 x body gives subst sigma1' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) body) = open_rec 0 (sigma1' x) (subst sigma1' body). sigma1' x = w1. Definitions:
```
Definition env := atom -> tm.
Fixpoint subst_env (sigma : env) (t : tm) : tm := ...
Definition env_update (sigma : env) (x : atom) (v : tm) : env :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then v else sigma y.
```

  Proof by induction on t; under abs, k increases, but the lc hyp still gives no bvars. Good.

Now, another key point: the fundamental theorem's E at opaque types (ind High) is trivial because V is True. But E's definition quantifies over evaluations; V True. Good.

Now, let's reconsider the "annotation bound" security_le fields in V for each type. Are they strong enough to guarantee the runtime flow checks in all eliminination rules, and are they preserved in all the operations?

Operations on V:
1. V_subtype (transport): runtime annotation ka stays; type annotation increases: security_le ka T ≤ security_le ka U by trans (Hle). ✓.
2. V at T_Abs construction: ka = type annotation; reflexive security_le. ✓.
3. V at Sum/Prod/Unit construction (T_Inl/T_Inr/T_Pair/T_Unit): runtime annotation = type annotation; reflexive. ✓.

Now V definition. Let's write it carefully:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          (exists k1 k2, v1 = tm_unit k1 /\ v2 = tm_unit k2 /\
                          security_le k1 k /\ security_le k2 k)
      end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          (exists a1 a2 ka1 ka2,
             v1 = tm_inl a1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 ka2 /\
             security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\ V T1 a1 a2)
          \/
          (exists a1 a2 ka1 ka2,
             v1 = tm_inr a1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 ka2 /\
             security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\ V T2 a1 a2)
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          (exists a1 a2 b1 b2 ka1 ka2,
             v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 ka2 /\
             security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\
             V T1 a1 a2 /\ V T2 b1 b2)
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          (exists Ta1 body1 ka1 Ta2 body2 ka2,
             v1 = tm_abs Ta1 body1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_abs Ta2 body2 ka2 /\
             security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\
             (forall w1 w2 r,
                value w1 -> value w2 -> V T1 w1 w2 ->
                flows_to k.(reader) r ->
                forall u1 u2,
                  tm_app v1 w1 r -->* u1 -> value u1 ->
                  tm_app v2 w2 r -->* u2 -> value u2 ->
                  V T2 u1 u2))
      end
  end.
```
Hmm, there's a potential guard-check issue: `V T1` and `V T2` occur inside the match arms on T; T1/T2 are variables bound by the outer match on T. Structural recursion should be accepted. But note in the arrow exists, we have `V T1 w1 w2` where T1 is a pattern variable from the outer match, and `V T2 u1 u2`. Both subterms. ✓.

Wait, the arrow clause's body uses v1 and v2 inside `tm_app v1 w1 r` while also asserting v1 = tm_abs ...; that's fine.

One issue: in the arrow case, the nested match on `k.(indirect_reader)` occurs; the recursive calls V T1, V T2 are inside. Fine.

Definition E (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> value v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v2 -> V T v1 v2.
```

Should work (rewrite under match on a variable reduces if H is a definitional equality of the scrutinee? `rewrite H` replaces occurrences of k.ind with High; the match scrutinee becomes High, then simpl reduces). - V_low_unfold lemmas for each constructor when ind Low: after `destruct k.ind` or with an equation, simpl gives the strict form. Might be easier to use `destruct (k.(indirect_reader)) eqn:Ek; simpl` in proofs.

Substitution definitions and lemmas:
```
Definition env := atom -> tm.
Definition env_update (sigma : env) (x : atom) (v : tm) : env :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then v else sigma y.

Fixpoint subst_env (sigma : env) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar y => sigma y
  ...
  end.
```
Note: this differs from the original `subst` (which is capture-free for a single atom). subst_env with sigma mapping x to t is essentially subst x t. For fvar y, sigma y (no eqb). Good.

Lemmas:
- env_update_same: env_update sigma x v x = v. unfold; rewrite Nat.eqb_refl; reflexivity.
- env_update_other: y <> x -> env_update sigma x v y = sigma y. unfold; rewrite Nat.eqb_neq by auto? Nat.eqb y x = false iff y ≠ x. `apply Nat.eqb_neq; auto` or `destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E; auto; apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; contradiction`.
- subst_env_lc: with pointwise lc.
- subst_env_ext: agreement on fv.
- subst_env_plug? As discussed, we only need a version with identity-off-x:
  `subst_env_plug_fresh : forall sigma C x t, sigma x = t -> (forall y, y <> x -> sigma y = tm_fvar y) -> ~ In x (fv_context C) -> subst_env sigma (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C t.`
  Define fv_context:
```
Fixpoint fv_context (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs _ C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t _ => fv_context C' ++ fv t
  | C_AppRight t C' _ => fv t ++ fv_context C'
  ...
```
This is a bit of work. Alternatively, prove a more general lemma with the agreement of sigma on all fvs of C:
```
Lemma subst_env_plug_agree : forall sigma C t,
  (forall y, In y (fv_context C) -> sigma y = tm_fvar y) ->
  subst_env sigma (plug C t) = plug C (subst_env sigma t).
```
Then in the final proof, need sigma identity on fv_context C. Our sigma is identity off x; so need x ∉ fv_context C. Since we choose x fresh from fv_context C. But we also need to know all fvs in C are... wait, sigma is identity for all y ≠ x globally, so if x ∉ fv_context C, then for any y ∈ fv_context C, y ≠ x, so sigma y = fvar y. ✓. So we only need x ∉ fv_context C, which we can choose from context_has_type's L? context_has_type gives "exists L, forall x ∉ L, typing". We choose x ∉ L ∪ fv_context C. ✓. No typing-fv analysis needed!

  But wait: the final env_rel needs lc of sigma y for all y. sigma off x = fvar y lc; at x t_i lc. ✓.

  And the fundamental theorem applied to plug C (fvar x) under sigma1 yields subst_env sigma1 (plug C (fvar x)) = plug C t1 by subst_env_plug_agree with sigma1 identity on fv_context C. ✓.

  We need fv_context and its lemmas; define it. Actually, we could avoid fv_context by using the list of all atoms? Not decidable. fv_context is fine.

  Alternatively, apply the fundamental theorem to the term `plug C (fvar x)` with substitutions that are identity off x; then subst_env sigma1 t = ... but the stored terms in C get substituted by sigma1; since sigma1 is identity off x and x ∉ fv C, they're unchanged. So subst_env_plug_agree with fv_context is the clean route.

  We need fv_context correctness: `fv (plug C t) = fv_context C ++ fv t` maybe not needed if we prove subst_env_plug_agree directly by induction on C. Let's prove directly:
```
Lemma subst_env_plug_agree : forall sigma C t,
  (forall y, In y (fv_context C) -> sigma y = tm_fvar y) ->
  subst_env sigma (plug C t) = plug C (subst_env sigma t).
Proof.
  intros sigma C; induction C; intros t Hagree; simpl in *; try (f_equal; eauto; fail).
```
Need to handle each constructor. For C_AppLeft C' s r: plug = tm_app (plug C' t) s r. LHS = tm_app (subst_env sigma (plug C' t)) (subst_env sigma s) r. RHS = tm_app (plug C' (subst_env sigma t)) s r. IH with agreement restricted to fv_context C' (subset of fv_context C): gives first components equal. For the second: need subst_env sigma s = s, which follows from Hagree on fv s (since fv_context (C_AppLeft C' s r) = fv_context C' ++ fv s). Use subst_env_ext with identity: (forall y, In y (fv s) -> sigma y = tm_fvar y) and subst_env_id: subst_env (fun y => tm_fvar y) s = s. Prove subst_env_id. Then rewrite.
  This is straightforward but verbose due to list app membership. Could define fv_context and prove `In y (fv_context C) -> ...` and use `subst_env_ext`.
  
  Maybe simpler: prove a stronger lemma: if sigma agrees with fvar on fvs of every stored term, then subst_env sigma (plug C t) = plug C (subst_env sigma t). Using subst_env_ext, for each stored term s, subst_env sigma s = subst_env id s = s when agreement holds on fv s. We'll write it.

  Alternatively, a much simpler approach: Since we can choose x fresh from ALL fvars of C and t1,t2, and the context typing places the hole... Could we instead substitute the *hole filling* syntactically with `subst x t1` (the original single-atom substitution)? The fundamental theorem uses subst_env. But we could prove a specialized theorem where the environment is a single-variable substitution? Hmm, subst_env with identity elsewhere equals subst x t1 (original subst) maybe. The original `subst x u t` substitutes only x (if eqb) and leaves others. Our sigma1 = fun y => if eqb y x then t1 else tm_fvar y = identity except x. Then subst_env sigma1 t = subst x t1 t provided t has no... actually subst_env sigma1 t substitutes every fvar y by sigma1 y = fvar y for y≠x, so it equals subst x t1 t definitionally-ish. And `subst x t1 (plug C (fvar x)) = plug C t1` if x is fresh w.r.t. the *bound-style fvars*? The original subst only touches fvars, and plug C (fvar x) has fvar x at the hole and possibly inside C; if x ∉ fv C, then subst x t1 (plug C (fvar x)) = plug C t1? Substitution under plug: subst x t1 (plug C (fvar x)) = plug C (subst x t1 (fvar x)) = plug C t1 only if C's stored terms contain no x (x ∉ fv C); otherwise subst changes them. Same as before. So we still need x ∉ fv C. That's easy to choose. Then the commuting lemma with subst and plug is the same.

  So either way, define fv_context or prove the plug-subst commutation with an fv-freshness hypothesis. Let's define fv_context (a straightforward Fixpoint) and prove subst_env_plug_agree.

Now, the fundamental theorem's env_rel with lc and E. Let's define:
```
Definition env_rel (Gamma : context) (sigma1 sigma2 : env) : Prop :=
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma1 y)) /\
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma2 y)) /\
  (forall y T, lookup_context y Gamma = Some T -> E T (sigma1 y) (sigma2 y)).
```

Now, in the fundamental T_Abs case, we need lookup lemmas:
- lookup_update_same: forall Gamma x T, lookup_context x (update Gamma x T) = Some T.
- lookup_update_other: forall Gamma x y T, y <> x -> lookup_context y (update Gamma x T) = lookup_context y Gamma.
Proof by unfold update, lookup_context; rewrite Nat.eqb_refl or Nat.eqb_neq.
- lookup_single: forall x T y U, lookup_context y [(x,T)] = Some U -> y = x /\ U = T. unfold lookup_context; destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E; intros H; try discriminate; apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; auto. Use `injection H` for U=T.

Then env_rel extension lemma:
```
Lemma env_rel_update : forall Gamma sigma1 sigma2 x T w1 w2,
  env_rel Gamma sigma1 sigma2 ->
  value w1 -> value w2 ->
  V T w1 w2 ->
  env_rel (update Gamma x T) (env_update sigma1 x w1) (env_update sigma2 x w2).
```
Proof: lc: point y; destruct (Nat.eqb y x): [value_lc w] or [env lc]. E: lookup y (update Gamma x T) = Some U. destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E.
  - true: y = x, U = T (need lookup_context x (update ...) = Some T; injectivity). Then goal E T (env_update sigma1 x w1 x) (env_update sigma2 x w2 x) = E T w1 w2, from V_to_E and values.
    Wait, lookup y (update Gamma x T) is by definition (if eqb y x then Some T else lookup y Gamma). If eqb y x true, the lookup value is Some T, so U = T. Good.
  - false: lookup y (update ...) = lookup y Gamma, apply env_rel.
Need value_lc: value v -> locally_closed v. Induction on value: unit: lc_at 0 (tm_unit k) = True. abs: hypothesis lc. pair: lc_at 0 (pair v1 v2 k) = lc_at 0 v1 ∧ lc_at 0 v2; IH. inl/inr: IH. ✓.

V_to_E:
```
Lemma V_to_E_value : forall T v1 v2, value v1 -> value v2 -> V T v1 v2 -> E T v1 v2.
Proof. intros T v1 v2 Hv1 Hv2 HV u1 u2 Hm1 Hval1 Hm2 Hval2.
  apply multi_value_eq in Hm1; [|exact Hv1|exact Hval1]... 
```
Wait multi_value_eq: value v1 -> multi v1 u1 -> value u1 -> u1 = v1. So rewrite Hm1, Hm2; exact HV.

Now the decomposition lemmas. Let's write them.

value inversion helpers:
```
Lemma value_app_absurd : forall a b r, ~ value (tm_app a b r).
Proof. intros; intro H; inversion H. Qed.
Lemma value_if_absurd, value_case_absurd, value_fst_absurd, value_snd_absurd, value_protect_absurd, value_bvar_absurd, value_fvar_absurd.
```
Actually inversion on value works for each; we can use `inversion H` directly in proofs. But some decomposition lemmas' refl cases need contradiction from value (app ...). We can state `value_app : value (tm_app a b r) -> False` etc. Let's define a general lemma by inversion:
```
Lemma value_inv_unit : forall v, value v -> ... 
```
Maybe simplest: for each term former, prove no value constructor. We'll write:
```
Lemma value_no_app : forall a b r, value (tm_app a b r) -> False.
Proof. intros; inversion H. Qed.
```
Similarly no_case, no_if, no_fst, no_snd, no_protect, no_bvar (no value constructor for bvar), no_fvar.
And inversion lemmas:
```
Lemma value_inv_pair : forall a b k, value (tm_pair a b k) -> value a /\ value b.
Proof. intros; inversion H; subst; auto. Qed.
```
But careful: inversion on value (tm_pair a b k) only matches v_pair with v1=a, v2=b, kappa=k; gives value a, value b. ✓.
```
Lemma value_inv_inl : forall a k, value (tm_inl a k) -> value a.
Lemma value_inv_inr.
Lemma value_inv_abs : forall T body k, value (tm_abs T body k) -> locally_closed (tm_abs T body k).
```
For the pair decomposition, we need that a step of a pair either steps a or b (and then multi continues), with values.

Let's write pair_multi_decomp:
```
Lemma pair_multi_decomp : forall a b k u,
  tm_pair a b k -->* u -> value u ->
  exists a' b', a -->* a' /\ value a' /\ b -->* b' /\ value b' /\ u = tm_pair a' b' k.
Proof.
  intros a b k u Hm. induction Hm as [| t1 t2 t3 Hstep Hm IH]; intros Hval.
  - (* refl *) exists a, b; split... but need value a, value b from value u = tm_pair a b k. Since Hm refl means u = t1? Let's see induction on multi: multi_refl case goal with u = tm_pair a b k and Hval : value (tm_pair a b k). value_inv_pair gives values. So exists a,b; multi_refl etc.; u = tm_pair a b k by refl.
  - (* step *) destruct Hstep via inversion:
    ST_Pair1: step a a', t2term = tm_pair a' b k. IH Hm Hval: exists a'' b'', a' -->* a'', ..., u = pair a'' b'' k. Then a -->* a'' by multi_step Hstep. exists a'', b''.
    ST_Pair2: value a, step b b'; t2 = pair a b' k; IH → a'' b''...; b -->* b'' by multi_step.
Qed.
```
Note: in the step case, `inversion Hstep; subst` handles the cases; but ST_Pair1/ST_Pair2 are the only rules producing a pair. There might also be other step rules matching tm_pair? None. So inversion.

For inl:
```
Lemma inl_multi_decomp : forall a k u, tm_inl a k -->* u -> value u ->
  exists a', a -->* a' /\ value a' /\ u = tm_inl a' k.
```
Steps: ST_Inl only. refl case: value (tm_inl a k) → value a.

Similarly inr, unit:
```
Lemma unit_multi_eq : forall k u, tm_unit k -->* u -> u = tm_unit k.
```
No step from unit: induction multi; refl; step contradiction. Could prove generally `multi_unit_inv`.
Abs:
```
Lemma abs_multi_eq : forall T body k u, tm_abs T body k -->* u -> u = tm_abs T body k.
```
Proof by induction on multi; step impossible (no step rule for abs as source). Need to invert step: step (tm_abs ...) t2 has no matching rule; `inversion Hstep`.
Similarly for bvar/fvar? not needed.

App decomp:
```
Lemma app_multi_decomp : forall a b r u,
  tm_app a b r -->* u -> value u ->
  exists a' b', a -->* a' /\ value a' /\ b -->* b' /\ value b' /\ tm_app a' b' r -->* u.
```
Induction on multi; refl case absurd (value app). Step cases ST_AppAbs/App1/App2.
- ST_AppAbs: a = tm_abs T body k; value abs; value v (b); flows; t2 = tm_protect k.ind (open body v). We know multi t2 u. Choose a' = tm_abs T body k, b' = v. Then a -->* a' (refl), value a', b -->* b' (refl), value b', and tm_app a' b' r -->* u: by multi_step (ST_AppAbs) Hm. ✓.
- ST_App1: step a a'; t2 = tm_app a' b r; IH Hm Hval gives a'' b''; a -->* a'' via multi_step; etc.
- ST_App2: value a; step b b'; t2 = tm_app a b' r; IH gives a'' b''; b -->* b'' via multi_step.

Fst decomp:

Lemma fst_multi_decomp : forall t r u,
  tm_fst t r -->* u -> value u ->
  exists t', t -->* t' /\ value t' /\ tm_fst t' r -->* u.
```
refl absurd (value fst). step: ST_FstPair → t = tm_pair v1 v2 k, value v1,v2, flows; t2 = tm_protect k.ind v1. choose t' = tm_pair v1 v2 k; t -->* t' refl; value pair; tm_fst t' r -->* u: multi_step ST_FstPair Hm. ST_Fst: t --> t'; IH. ✓.
Snd analogous.

Lemma protect_multi_decomp : forall l t u,

Better: induction on multi with the term fixed:

  induction Hm as [| t1 t2 t3 Hstep Hm IH].
  - (* refl: u = app abs w r; but u is... we don't have value u here. Hmm the lemma doesn't include value u; in refl case u = app...; conclusion tm_protect ... -->* app..., which is false in general. We need the value u hypothesis or use that the multi has at least one step. In the arrow behavioral clause, we have evaluations to a value u; the app term is not a value, so multi has at least one step. Let's state:
```
Lemma app_abs_multi : forall T body k w r u,
  value w -> flows_to k.(reader) r ->
  tm_app (tm_abs T body k) w r -->* u -> value u ->
  tm_protect k.(indirect_reader) (open body w) -->* u.
```
refl case: value u where u = app... contradiction (value_no_app). step case: invert the first step; since abs and w are values, the only possible step is ST_AppAbs (ST_App1 contradicts value_no_step abs; ST_App2 contradicts value w). Gives t2 = tm_protect k.ind (open body w); multi t2 u. ✓.
Actually ST_AppAbs requires value (tm_abs T body k): we don't have! The rule requires `value (tm_abs T body kappa)`. In the arrow behavioral clause, we have v1 = tm_abs Ta body ka and Hval1 : value v1 from E; so value of that abs. But in app_abs_multi we should include `value (tm_abs T body k)` as hypothesis too. Let's add it.
  In the arrow behavioral clause of V, we know v1 = tm_abs ... and E's Hval1 gives value v1 → value (tm_abs ...) after rewriting. So we can supply.
  Also ST_AppAbs requires value v (w) and flows_to k.reader r. ✓.
  ST_App1: step (tm_abs ...) t' → impossible by value_no_step with value abs.
  ST_App2: value (abs) then step w t' → impossible by value_no_step with value w.
So:

Lemma app_abs_multi : forall T body k w r u,
  value (tm_abs T body k) -> value w -> flows_to k.(reader) r ->

```

Lemma security_le_ind_low : forall ka kappa, security_le ka kappa -> kappa.(indirect_reader) = Low -> ka.(indirect_reader) = Low.

Now, the fundamental theorem. Let me write it in Coq and iterate. Before that, let's double check the tricky T_Abs env extension and rewriting.

T_Abs case details in Coq:

Context:
- Gamma : context, T1 : ty, body : tm, T2 : ty, kappa : security, L : list atom.
- Hbody : forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open body (tm_fvar x)) T2.
- IH : forall x (Hx : ~ In x L) sigma1 sigma2, env_rel (update Gamma x T1) sigma1 sigma2 -> E T2 (subst_env sigma1 (open body (tm_fvar x))) (subst_env sigma2 (open body (tm_fvar x))).
  (This depends on how induction generalizes; with `induction Hty` and motive `fun Gamma t T => forall sigma1 sigma2, env_rel Gamma sigma1 sigma2 -> E T (...)`, the T_Abs IH should be exactly this.)
- Goal: E (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) (subst_env sigma1 (tm_abs T1 body kappa)) (subst_env sigma2 (...)).
- Unfold E: intros v1 v2 Hm1 Hval1 Hm2 Hval2.
- assert (v1 = tm_abs T1 (subst_env sigma1 body) kappa) by eapply abs_multi_eq; exact Hm1. Note subst_env on abs is definitional: subst_env sigma (tm_abs T1 body kappa) = tm_abs T1 (subst_env sigma body) kappa, so Hm1 has the right type after simpl. Use `simpl in Hm1` maybe.
- Similarly v2.
- destruct (kappa.(indirect_reader)) eqn:Ek.

```
Fixpoint fresh (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | y :: ys => if Nat.eqb (fresh ys) y then S (fresh ys) else fresh ys  (* not quite guarantees *)
  end.
```
Simplest: `Definition fresh (l : list atom) := fold_right (fun y acc => max (S y) acc) 0 l.` Then prove fresh_not_in: ~ In (fresh l) l. With lia and list induction. Define:
```
Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with [] => 0 | y::ys => max (S y) (max_list ys) end.
Lemma max_list_gt : forall l y, In y l -> y < max_list l.
Proof. induction l; simpl; intros; [contradiction|]. destruct H; subst; lia. apply Nat.lt_le_trans with (max_list l); [apply IHl in H; lia | apply Nat.le_max_r]. Qed.
```

Lemma transparent_ground_erase : forall kappa T v1 v2,
  transparent_at kappa T ->
  ground T ->
  flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) Low ->
  V T v1 v2 ->
  erase_security v1 = erase_security v2.
Proof.
  intros kappa T. induction T as [k | T1 IH1 T2 IH2 k | T1 IH1 T2 IH2 k | T1 IH1 T2 IH2 k];
    intros v1 v2 Htr Hg Hflow HV; simpl in Hg.
```

Chunk 1:

```coq
(* ============================================================== *)
(* Helper lemmas about labels, security levels and values          *)
(* ============================================================== *)

Lemma flows_to_refl : forall l : label, flows_to l l.
Proof. destruct l; simpl; exact I. Qed.

Lemma flows_to_trans : forall l1 l2 l3 : label,
  flows_to l1 l2 -> flows_to l2 l3 -> flows_to l1 l3.
Proof. destruct l1, l2, l3; simpl in *; auto; contradiction. Qed.

Lemma security_le_refl : forall k : security, security_le k k.
Proof. intros [r i]; simpl; split; apply flows_to_refl. Qed.

Lemma security_le_trans : forall k1 k2 k3 : security,
  security_le k1 k2 -> security_le k2 k3 -> security_le k1 k3.
Proof.
  intros [r1 i1] [r2 i2] [r3 i3]; simpl; intros [H1 H2] [H3 H4].
  split; eapply flows_to_trans; eauto.
Qed.

Lemma security_le_reader_flow : forall (ka kb : security) (r : label),
  security_le ka kb -> flows_to kb.(reader) r -> flows_to ka.(reader) r.
Proof.
  intros [ra ia] [rb ib] r [Hr Hi] Hf; simpl in *.
  eapply flows_to_trans; eauto.
Qed.

Lemma security_le_ind_low : forall ka kb : security,
  security_le ka kb -> flows_to kb.(indirect_reader) Low ->
  ka.(indirect_reader) = Low.
Proof.
  intros [ra ia] [rb ib] [Hr Hi] Hf; simpl in *.
  destruct ia; auto.
  exfalso. exact (flows_to_trans _ _ _ Hi Hf).
Qed.

Lemma join_low_r : forall l : label, join l Low = l.
Proof. destruct l; reflexivity. Qed.
Lemma join_low_l : forall l : label, join Low l = l.
Proof. destruct l; reflexivity. Qed.
Lemma join_high_r : forall l : label, join l High = High.
Proof. destruct l; reflexivity. Qed.
Lemma join_high_l : forall l : label, join High l = High.
Proof. destruct l; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma protect_security_low : forall k : security, protect_security k Low = k.
Proof.
  intros [r i]; unfold protect_security; simpl.
  rewrite join_low_r, join_low_r. reflexivity.
Qed.

Lemma protect_security_high_ind : forall k : security,
  (protect_security k High).(indirect_reader) = High.
Proof.
  intros [r i]; unfold protect_security; simpl.
  rewrite join_high_r. reflexivity.
Qed.

Lemma ty_protect_low : forall T : ty, ty_protect Low T = T.
Proof.
  intros T; destruct T; simpl; rewrite protect_security_low; reflexivity.
Qed.

Lemma ty_protect_high_ind : forall T : ty,
  (security_of (ty_protect High T)).(indirect_reader) = High.
Proof.
  intros T; destruct T; simpl; rewrite protect_security_high_ind; reflexivity.
Qed.
```

Wait: protect_security_low proof: after `intros [r i]; unfold protect_security; simpl`, the goal might be `{|reader := join r Low; indirect_reader := join i Low|} = {|reader := r; indirect_reader := i|}`. `rewrite join_low_r` rewrites `join r Low` to r? The rewrite rule is forall l, join l Low = l, so it matches join r Low. Then join i Low. Then reflexivity. But `simpl` might already reduce the record projections and leave `{|...|} = {|...|}`; rewriting inside record notation should work. Alternatively `destruct r, i; reflexivity` after unfold. destruct on label fields: the record k is destructured as [r i]; r,i are labels; destruct r,i; simpl; reflexivity. Simpler:
```
Proof. intros [r i]; destruct r, i; reflexivity. Qed.
```
Since protect_security unfolds and joins compute on constructors. Let's use that. But `destruct r` in a goal with record fields—should work. Similarly join lemmas and high_ind:
```
Lemma protect_security_high_ind : forall k, (protect_security k High).(indirect_reader) = High.
Proof. intros [r i]; destruct i; reflexivity. Qed.
```
join i High = High for i=Low: join Low High = High; i=High: High. ✓.

Actually, there's an even simpler standard trick: prove the decomposition lemma by induction on the multi derivation *after reverting* the specific start expression and using the induction principle with a general start:
```
Lemma app_multi_decomp : forall a b r u, tm_app a b r -->* u -> value u -> ...
Proof.
  intros a b r u Hm.
  remember (tm_app a b r) as x eqn:Hx.
  revert a b r Hx.
  induction Hm as [| x1 x2 x3 Hstep Hm IH]; intros a b r Hx Hval; ...
```
With `remember`, Hx : x = tm_app a b r; then revert a b r Hx, so the IH is general. This is equivalent. The equation-in-statement approach is fine.

Actually, there's an alternative that avoids decomposition lemmas entirely: instead of proving E by considering arbitrary multi-steps, define E coinductively/semantically differently? For example, define `E T t1 t2` as: for all u1, t1 -->* u1 with value u1 implies exists u2, t2 -->* u2 ∧ value u2 ∧ V T u1 u2, plus converse. Then same_result needs both directions; E with existential only one direction might not suffice for equality when both evaluate. But our E is the "all pairs" version. The decomposition lemmas are needed to relate the evaluation of a compound term to evaluations of its parts. This is standard.

Alternatively, we can define V/E via a *big-step* relation and prove its equivalence with multi; big-step rules would make composition easier but we'd still need soundness/completeness w.r.t. multi. The decomposition lemmas are the standard route.

OK let's write the code. I'll be systematic. To reduce the risk of guard-check issues and complexity, maybe I should reconsider the definition of V: instead of requiring the abs's stored type arbitrary, etc. We'll see.

Lemma pair_multi_decomp : forall x u,
  multi x u -> value u ->
  forall a b k, x = tm_pair a b k ->
  exists a' b', a -->* a' /\ value a' /\ b -->* b' /\ value b' /\

Lemma app_multi_decomp : forall x u,

Lemma case_multi_decomp : forall x u,

Lemma if_multi_decomp : forall x u,
  multi x u -> value u -> forall c y n r, x = tm_if c y n r ->

Lemma protect_multi_decomp : forall x u,
  multi x u -> value u -> forall l t, x = tm_protect l t ->
  exists v, t -->* v /\ value v /\ u = protect_value v l.
Proof.

      assert (Heq : x3 = protect_value v0 l) by (eapply multi_value_eq; eauto).
      exists v0. repeat split; auto using multi_refl.
    + destruct (IH Hval l t0' eq_refl) as (v & Ht & Hv & Hu).
      exists v. repeat split; auto. eapply multi_step; eauto.
Qed.
```

Better alternative: Avoid inversion on step entirely by defining our own "head step" inversion lemmas with `inversion Hstep` inside and stating the possible outcomes as lemmas. For example, prove a lemma:

```
Lemma step_pair_inv : forall a b k x,
  step (tm_pair a b k) x ->
  (exists a', step a a' /\ x = tm_pair a' b k) \/
  (value a /\ exists b', step b b' /\ x = tm_pair a b' k).
Proof.
  intros a b k x H. inversion H; subst; auto.
  (* remaining possible cases *)
Qed.
```
Here inversion H (no as-pattern) leaves the possible cases; auto/eauto might solve using existential witnesses from the hypotheses. But the impossible cases: inversion closes them or leaves absurd hypotheses; `auto` may handle. Then in the decomposition lemmas, use this inversion lemma with destruct, and the names come from our own existential destructuring.

But proving step_*_inv lemmas themselves still requires handling inversion's remaining goals. Let's see for pair:
```
inversion H; subst.
- (* ST_Pair1 *) left; eexists; split; eauto.
- (* ST_Pair2 *) right; split; [eauto | eexists; split; eauto].
```
After inversion H with H : step (tm_pair a b k) x, does inversion leave only the two matching constructor cases? Inversion uses the unification of the conclusion with the start; cases that don't unify are discarded. Yes! That's how inversion normally works: it only keeps cases where the constructor's conclusion unifies with the given term; impossible ones are solved by discriminate. The "Expects a disjunctive pattern with 19 branches" error is specific to *as-patterns*: when you provide a pattern with fewer branches than constructors, Coq expects all constructors since it can't know which are impossible at pattern-parsing time. Without as, inversion leaves only the consistent cases. So `inversion H; subst` should leave exactly the 2 cases, with auto-generated names. Then I can use eexists and eauto to avoid naming.

Let me write the step inversion lemmas for the needed formers:

```
Lemma step_pair_inv : forall a b k x, step (tm_pair a b k) x ->
  (exists a', step a a' /\ x = tm_pair a' b k) \/
  (value a /\ exists b', step b b' /\ x = tm_pair a b' k).
Proof.
  intros a b k x H. inversion H; subst.
  - left. eauto.
  - right. eauto.
Qed.
```
Check: ST_Pair1 gives a --> t1' and x = tm_pair t1' b k. `left; eauto` should find exists a' := t1'. ST_Pair2 gives value v1 (which should be a after subst) and b --> t2' and x = tm_pair a t2' k. `right; eauto`. ✓.

Similarly:
```
Lemma step_inl_inv : forall a k x, step (tm_inl a k) x ->
  exists a', step a a' /\ x = tm_inl a' k.

```

  \/ (value a /\ exists b', step b b' /\ x = tm_app a b' r).
```

pair_multi_decomp:
```
Proof.
  intros x u Hm.
  induction Hm as [| x1 x2 x3 Hstep Hm IH]; intros Hval a b k Hx.
  - subst. destruct (value_inv_pair a b k Hval) as [Ha Hb].

  - subst x1. destruct (step_pair_inv a b k x2 Hstep) as
      [(a0' & Hstepa & Hx2) | (Hva & b0' & Hstepb & Hx2)].
    + subst x2. destruct (IH Hval a0' b k eq_refl) as (a'' & b'' & Ha & Hva' & Hb & Hvb' & Hu).
      exists a'', b''. repeat split; auto. eapply multi_step; eauto.
    + subst x2. destruct (IH Hval a b0' k eq_refl) as (a'' & b'' & Ha & Hva' & Hb & Hvb' & Hu).
      exists a'', b''. repeat split; auto. eapply multi_step; eauto.
Qed.
```
Note: in the first case, Hx2 : x2 = tm_pair a0' b k; `subst x2` works. IH's equation expects x2 = tm_pair a0' b k, but after subst x2, the IH is about the term tm_pair a0' b k? ```
+ destruct (IH Hval a0' b k Hx2) as (...). 
```
Then the result's Hu : tm_app... -->* x3. The goal's last component is about u (which equals x3). In the induction, the lemma's u is generalized to x3 in the step case? Let's see: with the new statement `forall x u, multi x u -> value u -> forall a b k, x = tm_pair a b k -> exists ... u = tm_pair a' b' k`, the induction motive at (x1,x3) has `value x3 -> forall a b k, x1 = tm_pair a b k -> exists ..., x3 = tm_pair a' b' k`. Substitution:

```coq
Definition env := atom -> tm.

Definition env_update (sigma : env) (x : atom) (v : tm) : env :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then v else sigma y.

Fixpoint subst_env (sigma : env) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar y => sigma y
  | tm_unit k => tm_unit k
  | tm_abs T body k => tm_abs T (subst_env sigma body) k
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_env sigma t1) (subst_env sigma t2) r
  | tm_pair t1 t2 k => tm_pair (subst_env sigma t1) (subst_env sigma t2) k
  | tm_fst t1 r => tm_fst (subst_env sigma t1) r
  | tm_snd t1 r => tm_snd (subst_env sigma t1) r
  | tm_inl t1 k => tm_inl (subst_env sigma t1) k
  | tm_inr t1 k => tm_inr (subst_env sigma t1) k
  | tm_case t0 b1 b2 r => tm_case (subst_env sigma t0) (subst_env sigma b1) (subst_env sigma b2) r
  | tm_protect l t1 => tm_protect l (subst_env sigma t1)
  | tm_if c y n r => tm_if (subst_env sigma c) (subst_env sigma y) (subst_env sigma n) r
  end.
```

fv_context:
```coq
Fixpoint fv_context (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs _ C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t _ => fv_context C' ++ fv t
  | C_AppRight t C' _ => fv t ++ fv_context C'
  | C_PairLeft C' t _ => fv_context C' ++ fv t
  | C_PairRight t C' _ => fv t ++ fv_context C'
  | C_Fst C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_Snd C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_Inl C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_Inr C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_CaseScrutinee C' t1 t2 _ => fv_context C' ++ fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | C_CaseLeft t0 C' t2 _ => fv t0 ++ fv_context C' ++ fv t2
  | C_CaseRight t0 t1 C' _ => fv t0 ++ fv t1 ++ fv_context C'
  | C_Protect _ C' => fv_context C'
  | C_IfCondition C' y n _ => fv_context C' ++ fv y ++ fv n
  | C_IfThen c C' n _ => fv c ++ fv_context C' ++ fv n
  | C_IfElse c y C' _ => fv c ++ fv y ++ fv_context C'
  end.
```

Lemma subst_env_ext : forall sigma sigma' t,
  (forall y, In y (fv t) -> sigma' y = sigma y) ->
  subst_env sigma' t = subst_env sigma t.
Proof.
  intros sigma sigma' t Hagree.
  induction t; simpl in *; try reflexivity; try (f_equal; eauto; fail).
```

For app/pair: f_equal; IH with fv t1 (In in fv t1 ++ fv t2); etc.
For fst/snd/inl/inr/protect: f_equal; IH.
For case/if: f_equal; IH each; membership via In_app.

Lemma subst_env_id_on_fv : forall sigma s,
  (forall y, In y (fv s) -> sigma y = tm_fvar y) -> subst_env sigma s = s.

```
Lemma subst_env_id_on_fv : forall sigma s,
  (forall y, In y (fv s) -> sigma y = tm_fvar y) -> subst_env sigma s = s.
Proof.
  intros sigma s H.
  transitivity (subst_env (fun y => tm_fvar y) s).
  - apply subst_env_ext. intros y Hy. symmetry. apply H; auto.
  - apply subst_env_id.
Qed.
```

Lemma subst_env_plug_agree : forall sigma C t,

The subst_env_plug_agree approach directly proves what we need without typing lemmas about fv. It requires induction over C with membership handling. Let's just do it. Actually, we can simplify by proving a stronger lemma where sigma is identity on *all* atoms (the identity substitution) gives subst_env id (plug C t) = plug C t? Not helpful.

Alternative trick: Define the substitutions sigma1, sigma2 not as identity-off-x but as functions that agree with identity on all atoms except x, and note that subst_env sigma (plug C (fvar x)) = plug C (subst_env sigma (fvar x)) holds *by induction on C without any fv hypothesis* if we choose sigma to be identity except x and C contains no x? The induction fails on stored terms only if they contain x. If C contains no fvar x, then sigma acts as identity on stored terms by definition (sigma y = fvar y for y ≠ x), regardless of other fvars. So we need: sigma is identity on all y ≠ x (not just fv C), and x ∉ fv C. Then subst_env sigma s = s for any s with x ∉ fv s (even if s has other fvars), because sigma y = fvar y for y ≠ x. So the agreement hypothesis reduces to x ∉ fv of stored terms = x ∉ fv_context C. We can choose x ∉ fv_context C ∪ L. Then:
- For any stored term s in C, x ∉ fv s; subst_env sigma s = s by subst_env_id_on_fv with the hypothesis y ∈ fv s → y ≠ x → sigma y = fvar y.
Thus the lemma `subst_env_plug_x`:
```
Lemma subst_env_plug_x : forall sigma C t x,
  sigma x = t ->
  (forall y, y <> x -> sigma y = tm_fvar y) ->
  ~ In x (fv_context C) ->
  subst_env sigma (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C t.
```
Proof by induction C; the membership x ∉ fv_context C implies x ∉ fv s for each stored s (via fv_context membership), and sigma identity off x gives subst_env sigma s = s using subst_env_id_on_fv (needs: for y ∈ fv s, sigma y = fvar y; since y ≠ x because x ∉ fv s, apply Hident). This is clean and avoids a general agreement lemma. But we still need induction over C with per-constructor handling; similar effort. The advantage: the per-stored-term goal is discharged by a uniform tactic using a lemma about fv_context membership.

We still need `subst_env_id_on_fv`, which needs `subst_env_ext` and `subst_env_id`.

Hmm, is there an even simpler route to avoid subst_env/plug entirely? Let me write V as planned. Need to be careful about Coq's guard condition and the `match k.(indirect_reader)`.

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low => exists k1 k2, v1 = tm_unit k1 /\ v2 = tm_unit k2 /\
                 security_le k1 k /\ security_le k2 k
      end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          (exists a1 a2 ka1 ka2, v1 = tm_inl a1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 ka2 /\
             security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\ V T1 a1 a2)
          \/
          (exists a1 a2 ka1 ka2, v1 = tm_inr a1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 ka2 /\
             security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\ V T2 a1 a2)
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          exists a1 a2 b1 b2 ka1 ka2,
            v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 ka2 /\
            security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\ V T1 a1 a2 /\ V T2 b1 b2
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 k =>
      match k.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          exists Ta1 body1 ka1 Ta2 body2 ka2,
            v1 = tm_abs Ta1 body1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_abs Ta2 body2 ka2 /\
            security_le ka1 k /\ security_le ka2 k /\
            (forall w1 w2 r, value w1 -> value w2 -> V T1 w1 w2 ->
               flows_to k.(reader) r ->
               forall u1 u2,
                 tm_app v1 w1 r -->* u1 -> value u1 ->
                 tm_app v2 w2 r -->* u2 -> value u2 ->
                 V T2 u1 u2)
      end
  end.
```
Potential issue: in the arrow branch, `tm_app v1 w1 r` uses v1 which is bound outside the exists; fine. But inside the exists we also state v1 = tm_abs...; the quantification over app v1 is on the original v1. Good.

Now Coq's guard: V appears in V T1, V T2 inside the match on T; T1,T2 are subterms in the pattern. Should be structurally decreasing.

But wait: in the arrow branch, the `exists ...` includes T1 and T2 from the match; V T1 and V T2 recursive calls are on pattern variables. Coq's guard checker with nested matches on `k.ind` (a projection of a variable from the outer match) should still see T1/T2 as subterms. I believe yes.

Definition E (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> value v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v2 -> V T v1 v2.

Lemma V_to_E_value : forall T v1 v2,

env_rel:
```
Definition env_rel (Gamma : context) (sigma1 sigma2 : env) : Prop :=
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma1 y)) /\
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma2 y)) /\
  (forall y T, lookup_context y Gamma = Some T -> E T (sigma1 y) (sigma2 y)).
```

env_rel_update:
```
Lemma env_rel_update : forall Gamma sigma1 sigma2 x T w1 w2,
  env_rel Gamma sigma1 sigma2 ->
  value w1 -> value w2 -> V T w1 w2 ->
  env_rel (update Gamma x T) (env_update sigma1 x w1) (env_update sigma2 x w2).
Proof.
  intros Gamma sigma1 sigma2 x T w1 w2 (Hlc1 & Hlc2 & HE) Hv1 Hv2 HV.
  split.
  - intros y. unfold env_update. destruct (Nat.eqb y x); [apply value_lc; auto | apply Hlc1].
  - split.
    + intros y. unfold env_update. destruct (Nat.eqb y x); [apply value_lc; auto | apply Hlc2].
    + intros y U Hlook.
      unfold update in Hlook.
      destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:Ey.
      * apply Nat.eqb_eq in Ey. subst y.
        injection Hlook as HU. subst U.
        unfold env_update. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl.
        apply V_to_E_value; auto.
      * unfold env_update. rewrite Ey.
        apply HE. exact Hlook.
Qed.
```
Careful: `update Gamma x T` is definitionally `(x,T)::Gamma`, and lookup_context y ((x,T)::Gamma) = if Nat.eqb y x then Some T else lookup_context y Gamma. The unfold should work. Hlook after unfold update: lookup_context y ((x,T)::Gamma) = Some U. simp. `destruct (Nat.eqb y x)`. In the true case, Hlook : Some T = Some U; injection. Good.

Part A: env, subst_env, fv_context, subst lemmas.

Let me write carefully.

```coq
(* ============================================================== *)
(* Substitutions                                                   *)
(* ============================================================== *)

Definition env := atom -> tm.

Definition env_update (sigma : env) (x : atom) (v : tm) : env :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then v else sigma y.

Lemma env_update_same : forall sigma x v, env_update sigma x v x = v.
Proof. intros; unfold env_update; rewrite Nat.eqb_refl; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma env_update_other : forall sigma x y v, y <> x -> env_update sigma x v y = sigma y.
Proof.
  intros sigma x y v Hneq. unfold env_update.
  destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E; auto.
  apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; contradiction.
Qed.

Fixpoint subst_env (sigma : env) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar y => sigma y
  | tm_unit k => tm_unit k
  | tm_abs T body k => tm_abs T (subst_env sigma body) k
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_env sigma t1) (subst_env sigma t2) r
  | tm_pair t1 t2 k => tm_pair (subst_env sigma t1) (subst_env sigma t2) k
  | tm_fst t1 r => tm_fst (subst_env sigma t1) r
  | tm_snd t1 r => tm_snd (subst_env sigma t1) r
  | tm_inl t1 k => tm_inl (subst_env sigma t1) k
  | tm_inr t1 k => tm_inr (subst_env sigma t1) k
  | tm_case t0 b1 b2 r => tm_case (subst_env sigma t0) (subst_env sigma b1) (subst_env sigma b2) r
  | tm_protect l t1 => tm_protect l (subst_env sigma t1)
  | tm_if c y n r => tm_if (subst_env sigma c) (subst_env sigma y) (subst_env sigma n) r
  end.

Fixpoint fv_context (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs _ C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_AppLeft C' s _ => fv_context C' ++ fv s
  | C_AppRight s C' _ => fv s ++ fv_context C'
  | C_PairLeft C' s _ => fv_context C' ++ fv s
  | C_PairRight s C' _ => fv s ++ fv_context C'
  | C_Fst C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_Snd C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_Inl C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_Inr C' _ => fv_context C'
  | C_CaseScrutinee C' s1 s2 _ => fv_context C' ++ fv s1 ++ fv s2
  | C_CaseLeft s0 C' s2 _ => fv s0 ++ fv_context C' ++ fv s2
  | C_CaseRight s0 s1 C' _ => fv s0 ++ fv s1 ++ fv_context C'
  | C_Protect _ C' => fv_context C'
  | C_IfCondition C' s1 s2 _ => fv_context C' ++ fv s1 ++ fv s2
  | C_IfThen s0 C' s2 _ => fv s0 ++ fv_context C' ++ fv s2
  | C_IfElse s0 s1 C' _ => fv s0 ++ fv s1 ++ fv_context C'
  end.

Lemma subst_env_id : forall t, subst_env (fun y => tm_fvar y) t = t.
Proof.
  induction t; simpl; try reflexivity; try (f_equal; assumption).
Qed.
```

The induction binder pattern for tm_abs is wrong. The constructor tm_abs : ty -> tm -> security -> tm. In the induction pattern I wrote `| T body s IHbody |` which binds T, body, s as the arguments and then IHbody as the induction hypothesis. But Coq's induction binder pattern order for a constructor is: arguments first, then IH(s). tm_abs has 3 args: ty, tm, security. So the pattern should be `T body s IHbody`, which I wrote. But the error shows IHbody : security, s : (forall...) -> ...; meaning Coq bound the pattern differently: it treated `T body` as args and then `s IHbody`... Hmm, Coq's `induction t as [ ... | T body s IHbody | ...]` for tm_abs: it expects the args T body s, then one IH per recursive argument (the tm body). The pattern listing should be `T body s IHbody` where IHbody is the IH. But the error suggests it assigned: T : ty, body : tm, IHbody : security, s : IH. So Coq expects the IH *before* the non-recursive args?
